### The Five Shifts of Modern Change Practice

**Source:** "Change needs changing" blog | transformationleadership.institute | Section: "What needs to change in change — The 5 Shifts"

**Verbatim transcript quote:**
> "The old playbook of linear plans, communications, and training, along with methodology debates, can't carry the speed, scale, and messiness that organisations are already facing. Here's what I'm seeing, and what actually helps."
> "Move from 'stakeholders' to specific humans in specific jobs. Map the few critical behaviours by role and moment. Measure usable adoption (is the new way used in the flow of work? If not, why?)"
> "Turn concepts into micro-practices people can try today. Use AI for the unglamorous heavy lifting: segment communications by role, draft job-specific 'first-run' guides, summarise feedback channels, and surface friction signals in real-time. AI is not the change; it's the amplifier."

**What this IP is:**
A five-part framework that replaces the outdated change management playbook with a modern, integrated approach. Each shift addresses a specific failure mode in conventional practice. Together they form a complete operating upgrade for any change or transformation leader who wants their work to actually land and stick — not just be delivered.

**Who it is for:**
Senior change and transformation practitioners who are frustrated that their technically correct work is not producing adoption. Programme leaders who are running conventional change plans and watching them underperform. Executives who have funded change and are not seeing the results they were promised.

**Pain point it addresses:**
The conventional change playbook — stakeholder maps, communications calendars, training sessions, methodology debates — is not producing adoption at the speed and scale organisations now need. Practitioners know something is wrong but are not sure what to replace it with. The result is good people doing the right things in the wrong sequence, at the wrong level of personalisation, without the culture levers that make change stick.

**Benefits / value add:**
The Five Shifts give practitioners a concrete upgrade path. Each shift is actionable, not aspirational. When embedded, they increase adoption rates significantly — Jess cites approximately 40% adoption increase in programmes that embed these moves. They also reduce transformation friction and give practitioners a language for explaining why their approach is different from the conventional model.

**The angle:**
The counterintuitive insight is that the problem is not effort — it is architecture. Most change practitioners are working very hard using the wrong design. The Five Shifts are not about doing more; they are about redesigning what you do. The most provocative shift is Shift 4: the distinction between having a change professional on the programme from day one versus having change adoption design embedded from day one. Jess calls this "like night and day" — and most practitioners who read it will recognise they have been doing the former while believing they were doing the latter.

**The framework:**

Shift 1 — Hyper-personalise adoption. Move from "stakeholders" as a category to specific humans in specific jobs. Map the few critical behaviours by role and moment. Measure usable adoption: is the new way actually being used in the flow of work? If not, why not? This shift moves change from a broadcast activity to a precision intervention.

Shift 2 — Fewer concepts, more practice, plus AI where it helps. Turn concepts into micro-practices people can try today — not in a training room, but in the actual flow of work. Use AI for the unglamorous heavy lifting: segment communications by role, draft job-specific "first-run" guides, summarise feedback channels, surface friction signals in real-time. The critical distinction: AI is not the change. It is the amplifier. This prevents the common mistake of treating AI adoption as the transformation goal.

Shift 3 — No change without culture, no culture without change. Make three non-negotiable cultural behaviours explicit. Tie them to decisions, rituals, and consequences. No change will ever be adopted if the culture has not shifted. And no culture will ever evolve without intentional change interventions that address culture directly. These two disciplines must be integrated, not sequential.

Shift 4 — Start change on day one, not close to go-live. Embed adoption design in discovery. Co-create ways of working with the people who will live them. Replace long-form training with in-flow enablement and micro-learnings: pilots, shadowing, checklists at the moment of need, quick feedback loops. The critical distinction (Jess's own emphasis): having a change professional on the programme from day one is not the same as having change adoption and change experience design embedded in the programme from day one. This distinction is like night and day.

Shift 5 — Build capability, not just awareness. Upskill people's capabilities — not just technical training, but also behavioural: navigating complex environments, protecting boundaries and wellbeing. Grow organisational capabilities that remove friction (data literacy, decision rights, customer journey view) so the change does not grind against the system.

**How it can be reused:**
- **Workshops:** The Five Shifts is a complete workshop module. Each shift can be a 20-minute deep-dive with a diagnostic question and a practical exercise.
- **Talks:** A keynote structured around "what has gone stale and what actually helps" — the Five Shifts as the answer.
- **The book:** A chapter titled "The Five Shifts" — the upgrade from the old playbook to the modern practice architecture.
- **Online content:** A LinkedIn post series (one per shift, five posts) with a practical diagnostic question at the end of each.
- **Client work:** Use as a programme design audit — score the current programme against each shift and identify the gaps.
- **GTL Programme:** Shift 4 (the day-one distinction) is a core module in the practice pillar.

**Soundbite:**
> "AI is not the change. It's the amplifier."

**Practical application:**
Run a 10-minute audit using Jess's own checklist from the blog: Who must do what differently by when (one sentence per role)? Where does the old habit still win? What tolerated behaviour makes candour unsafe? Which two micro-practices will help this week? How will you see adoption in the wild (three signals)? This audit can be run by a practitioner alone, with a sponsor, or with a team.

**Tips, nuances, and edge cases:**
- Shift 4 is the most commonly misunderstood. Many practitioners believe they are doing it because they are "on the programme from day one." The test is whether adoption design — not just change presence — is embedded in the discovery and shaping phases.
- Shift 3 requires naming specific behaviours, not values. "Psychological safety" is not a behaviour. "Decide in the room and say the hard thing early" is a behaviour.
- Shift 2's AI guidance is deliberately narrow: AI for the unglamorous heavy lifting, not for the human work. This is a positioning distinction that matters when speaking to sceptical executives.

**Domain tags:**
Practice — specifically the architecture of modern change practice; also touches Positioning (how a practitioner explains their approach) and Leadership (culture and behaviour design)

**Connections to other IP:**
- Blog 10 ("Change Management is boring") — the Five Shifts are the answer to the credibility problem described there. The two blogs form a paired argument: here is what is broken, here is how to fix it.
- Cheryl Session 1 — "Don't be the arms and legs" connects to Shift 4: being embedded in design, not just supporting delivery.
- Blog 5 ("The missing layer in your 2026 plan") — the Conditions of Success Runway is the programme-level expression of the same principles the Five Shifts address at the practitioner level.

**LinkedIn post idea:**

Story: "I've been watching the same patterns repeat across organisations for years. Stakeholder maps that don't move people. Training sessions that don't change behaviour. Communications calendars that tick boxes but don't shift anything. The problem isn't effort. It's architecture."
Insight: "Here are the five shifts that actually work: hyper-personalise adoption, fewer concepts and more practice, no change without culture, start on day one (not go-live), and build capability not just awareness. The most important one? The distinction between having a change professional on the programme from day one and having change adoption design embedded from day one. That distinction is like night and day."
CTA: "Which of these five shifts is your programme missing? Comment below — I read every response."

### The Day-One Distinction — Change Presence vs. Change Design

**Source:** "Change needs changing" blog | Section: Shift 4 | transformationleadership.institute

**Verbatim transcript quote:**
> "If you have read the title of this point, #4, and you already know you are doing this (engaging change early), I invite you to look deeper into the distinction between having a change professional/team on the program from day 1 and having change adoption and change experience design embedded in the program from day 1. This distinction is like night and day."

**What this IP is:**
A precision distinction that separates two things that look identical from the outside but produce completely different results. Having a change professional present from day one is a staffing decision. Having change adoption design embedded from day one is an architectural decision. Most programmes do the former and believe they are doing the latter.

**Who it is for:**
Change directors and programme leads who are confident they are doing change "right" because they have a change team on the programme from the start. Executives who are sponsoring transformation and want to understand why their change investment is not producing the adoption they expected.

**Pain point it addresses:**
The frustration of doing everything "correctly" — having change resources, running the workshops, producing the plans — and still not seeing adoption. The distinction explains why: presence is not the same as design. The change team is there, but the adoption architecture is not embedded in the programme's shaping and discovery phases.

**Benefits / value add:**
This distinction gives practitioners a precise language for a conversation they have been unable to have. It also gives sponsors a diagnostic question: "Is our change team present, or is change adoption design embedded?" The answer to that question predicts the programme's adoption outcome more accurately than any other single factor.

**The angle:**
The counterintuitive insight is that having a change team from day one can actually create a false sense of security. The organisation believes it has "done change" because the change team is there. But if adoption design is not embedded in the discovery and shaping phases, the change team is just watching the problem being created rather than preventing it.

**The framework:**
Two states: (1) Change presence — a change professional or team is on the programme from day one, attending meetings, producing plans, running stakeholder maps. (2) Change design embedded — adoption is designed into the programme architecture from discovery: who must do what differently is mapped before the solution is built; the culture and behaviour levers are identified before go-live; the in-flow enablement is designed before training is scheduled. The test: if the change team were removed from the programme tomorrow, would the adoption design still be embedded in the programme's architecture? If yes, you have done Shift 4. If no, you have change presence, not change design.

**How it can be reused:**
- **Diagnostic tool:** Use as a single-question diagnostic in a programme health check.
- **Talks:** A 5-minute segment in any keynote on transformation practice.
- **Client work:** Use to reframe a conversation with a sponsor who believes change is "covered" because there is a change team.
- **GTL Programme:** A core module in the Practice pillar — the distinction between being on the programme and being embedded in the programme.

**Soundbite:**
> "Having a change professional on the programme from day one and having change adoption design embedded from day one — that distinction is like night and day."

**Practical application:**
Ask three questions: (1) Is adoption designed into the discovery phase, or is it planned for later? (2) Are the critical behaviours by role identified before the solution is built, or after? (3) Is the culture work integrated with the delivery work, or is it a separate workstream? If the answer to any of these is "after" or "separate," the programme has change presence, not change design.

**Tips, nuances, and edge cases:**
- This distinction is most powerful when used with a sponsor who is resistant to investing in change. It reframes the conversation from "do you have change resources?" to "is change designed in?"
- The distinction also applies to culture work: having a culture programme running alongside transformation is not the same as having culture designed into the transformation.

**Domain tags:**
Practice — the architecture of change adoption design; also Positioning (how practitioners explain their value)

**Connections to other IP:**
- Blog 5 ("The missing layer") — the Conditions of Success Runway is the programme-level version of this distinction. Component 3 (Change Enablement) is where this distinction lives at the programme design level.
- Blog 10 ("Change Management is boring") — Pattern 1 ("Change as a companion to delivery") is the organisational expression of the same problem this distinction addresses at the practitioner level.

**LinkedIn post idea:**
Hook: "You have a change team on the programme from day one. That's not the same as having change designed in."
Story: "I've seen this pattern more times than I can count. The change team is present. They're attending the meetings. They're producing the plans. And yet, when the programme reaches go-live, adoption is lower than expected. The reason is almost always this distinction."
Insight: "Having a change professional present from day one is a staffing decision. Having change adoption design embedded from day one is an architectural decision. The test: if the change team were removed tomorrow, would the adoption design still be in the programme? If not, you have presence, not design."
CTA: "Which one does your programme have? Be honest."

### AI as Amplifier, Not the Change

**Source:** "Change needs changing" blog | Section: Shift 2 | transformationleadership.institute

**Verbatim transcript quote:**
> "Use AI for the unglamorous heavy lifting: segment communications by role, draft job-specific 'first-run' guides, summarise feedback channels, and surface friction signals in real-time. AI is not the change; it's the amplifier."

**What this IP is:**
A positioning statement and practical framework for how AI fits into change and transformation work. It prevents two common mistakes: treating AI adoption as the transformation goal, and ignoring AI as a practical tool for the unglamorous work that consumes change practitioners' time.

**Who it is for:**
Change and transformation practitioners who are uncertain how to position AI in their work. Executives who are being sold AI transformation programmes and want a framework for evaluating them. Practitioners who are spending too much time on communications, segmentation, and feedback synthesis and want to reclaim that time for the human work.

**Pain point it addresses:**
The confusion about AI's role in transformation. Some organisations are treating AI adoption as the transformation itself. Others are ignoring AI entirely. Both are mistakes. The "amplifier" framing gives practitioners a precise position: AI handles the unglamorous heavy lifting so practitioners can focus on the human work that AI cannot do.

**Benefits / value add:**
Practitioners who adopt this framing can reclaim significant time from the unglamorous work (communications segmentation, feedback synthesis, guide drafting) and redirect it to the high-value human work (stakeholder conversations, culture interventions, leadership coaching). They also have a clear, defensible position when executives ask about AI's role in the programme.

**The angle:**
The counterintuitive insight is that the most valuable thing AI can do for change practitioners is the work that feels least important — the segmentation, the drafting, the synthesis. By doing that work faster and better, AI frees the practitioner for the work that actually moves people: the conversations, the coaching, the culture work.

**The framework:**
AI's role in change practice: (1) Segment communications by role — not one message to all, but role-specific communications drafted at scale. (2) Draft job-specific "first-run" guides — the practical "what do I do differently on Monday morning" guides that practitioners never have time to write. (3) Summarise feedback channels — synthesise resistance signals, questions, and friction points from multiple channels into actionable themes. (4) Surface friction signals in real-time — identify where adoption is stalling before it becomes a crisis. What AI does NOT do: the human work of building trust, navigating politics, coaching leaders, and designing culture interventions.

**How it can be reused:**
- **Talks:** A 3-minute segment in any keynote on AI and transformation.
- **Online content:** A LinkedIn post on "what AI can and cannot do in change work."
- **Client work:** Use to scope AI's role in a programme and prevent the common mistake of treating AI adoption as the transformation goal.
- **GTL Programme:** A module in the Practice pillar on AI-enabled change practice.

**Soundbite:**
> "AI is not the change. It's the amplifier."

**Practical application:**
Identify the three most time-consuming unglamorous tasks in your current change work. Ask: can AI do this faster and better than I can? If yes, delegate it. Reclaim that time for the human work that only you can do.

**Tips, nuances, and edge cases:**
- The "amplifier" framing is also a positioning tool when speaking to executives who are sceptical of AI in change work. It positions AI as a productivity tool, not a replacement for the practitioner.
- The risk to flag: AI can produce generic communications and guides that feel impersonal. The practitioner's role is to review and calibrate the AI output, not just publish it.

**Domain tags:**
Practice — AI-enabled change practice; also Positioning (how practitioners explain their approach to AI)

**Connections to other IP:**
- Blog 3 ("So What Is Transformation Again?") — the AI readiness insight in that blog connects here: AI transformation fails when the business foundations are not ready. The "amplifier" framing assumes the foundations are in place.

**LinkedIn post idea:**
Hook: "AI is not the change. It's the amplifier."
Story: "I keep seeing two mistakes. Organisations treating AI adoption as the transformation itself. And practitioners ignoring AI entirely because it feels like a distraction from the real work. Both are wrong."
Insight: "AI's job in change work is the unglamorous heavy lifting: segment your communications by role, draft the job-specific guides, synthesise the feedback channels, surface the friction signals. That frees you for the work AI cannot do — the conversations, the coaching, the culture work."
CTA: "What unglamorous task are you still doing manually that AI could do in 10 minutes? I'll give you the prompt."

## LINKEDIN POSTS — READY TO SCHEDULE

### Post 1 — "The Five Shifts" (Carousel / Series Opener)

**Hook:**
The old change playbook is stale. Here are the 5 shifts that actually move adoption.

**Body:**
The world moved. Expectations are higher. Time is tighter. People are tired.

And yet most change programmes are still running the same playbook: methodology debates, linear plans, comms and training, and a change team that gets called in after the decisions are made.

Here are the 5 shifts I see in the programmes that actually land:

1. Hyper-personalise adoption. Not "stakeholders." Specific humans in specific jobs. What must they do differently? When? What gets in the way?

2. Fewer concepts, more practice. Turn your frameworks into micro-practices people can try today. AI handles the unglamorous heavy lifting — segment comms, draft role-specific guides, surface friction signals. That frees you for the human work.

3. No change without culture. Three non-negotiable behaviours. Tied to decisions, rituals, and consequences. Culture doesn't change by accident.

4. Start change on day one. Not close to go-live. There's a critical distinction between having a change team present from day one and having change adoption designed in from day one. That distinction predicts outcomes more than anything else.

5. Build capability, not just awareness. Technical, behavioural, navigational. Grow the organisational capabilities that remove friction so the change doesn't grind against the system.

Programmes that embed these five moves increase adoption by around 40%. Not magic. Internal programme design that matches reality.

**CTA:**
Which of these five is your biggest gap right now? Tell me in the comments — I'll give you one practical move.

---

### Post 2 — "The Day-One Distinction" (Standalone)

**Hook:**
There's a difference between having a change team present from day one and having change adoption designed in from day one.

That distinction is like night and day.

**Body:**
I keep seeing this play out.

A change lead is brought in at the start of a programme. They're in the room. They're on the plan. The sponsor says "we've got change covered from the beginning."

But what's actually happening is presence, not design.

The change team is attending delivery meetings. They're not shaping the conditions of success. They're not designing the adoption architecture. They're not asking: who must do what differently, by when, and what will stop them?

That work — the real work — gets deferred until "closer to go-live."

And then everyone wonders why adoption is hard.

The question to ask your programme lead: "Is change adoption designed into the discovery phase, or is it being planned for later?"

If the answer is "later" — you already know what's coming.

**CTA:**
If you're a sponsor or programme lead, ask that question this week. If you're a change practitioner, this is the conversation you need to be having on day one — not the one about your role on the RACI.

---

### Post 3 — "AI as Amplifier" (Short / Punchy)

**Hook:**
AI is not the change. It's the amplifier.

**Body:**
I keep seeing two mistakes.

Organisations treating AI adoption as the transformation itself. And practitioners ignoring AI entirely because it feels like a distraction from the real work.

Both are wrong.

AI's job in change work is the unglamorous heavy lifting: segment your communications by role, draft the job-specific first-run guides, synthesise the feedback channels, surface the friction signals in real time.

That frees you for the work AI cannot do: the conversations, the coaching, the culture work, the hard human stuff.

The amplifier only works if the signal is strong. Make sure the foundations are in place first.

**CTA:**
What unglamorous task are you still doing manually that AI could do in 10 minutes? Drop it in the comments — I'll give you the prompt.

### Transformation as Reality Creation

**Source:** "So What Is Transformation Again?" blog | Section: "What transformation is (and is not)" | transformationleadership.institute

**Verbatim transcript quote:**
> "Transformation is a reality creation journey. You take a future worth having and make it real, adopted, and sustained, not just launched."

**What this IP is:**
The cleanest, most powerful definition of transformation Jess has articulated. It is not a project definition, not a methodology definition, and not a change management definition. It is a purpose definition: transformation exists to create a new reality, not to deliver a new system. The three-word test — real, adopted, and sustained — is a complete success framework in itself.

**Who it is for:**
Any transformation or change leader who has struggled to explain what they do in a way that lands with executives, boards, or clients. Practitioners who are tired of being confused with project managers. Executives who are funding "transformation" and not seeing the results they expected.

**Pain point it addresses:**
The word "transformation" has been so diluted that it has lost meaning in many organisations. It is used to describe everything from a system implementation to a restructure to a branding refresh. This dilution has a direct cost: under-scoping, under-resourcing, and under-delivering. Practitioners who cannot define transformation precisely cannot defend their mandate, their budget, or their compensation.

**Benefits / value add:**
A precise, memorable definition that can be used in any conversation — with a board, a sponsor, a client, or a hiring manager. The "real, adopted, and sustained" test gives anyone a three-question audit for any transformation programme: Is it real (not just launched)? Is it adopted (not just trained)? Is it sustained (not just maintained)?

**The angle:**
The counterintuitive insight is that most "transformation" programmes are not transformations at all — they are delivery programmes with change support added on top. The definition "reality creation journey" is a direct challenge to that framing. It redefines the success criterion from "launched" to "real, adopted, and sustained." That shift in success criterion changes everything: the scope, the resourcing, the timeline, the leadership behaviours required.

**The framework:**
Transformation is a reality creation journey. To create a new reality, three things must happen:

1. **Prepare the soil.** Every transformation lives or dies depending on the culture it is planted in — the beliefs, mindset, incentives, ways of working, decision-making processes, the level of humaneness in the workplace, and power dynamics. This is the soil. You cannot plant a new reality in soil that will reject it.

2. **Build people and business capabilities.** The human and enterprise muscles that deliver outcomes repeatedly and can evolve their own capabilities over time. Not just technical skills — the behavioural, navigational, and adaptive capabilities that allow the organisation to keep changing after the programme ends.

3. **Shift the trajectory.** Change the nature of results, not just the volume of activity. Measured not just financially but also in how customers describe the brand and how employees talk about their workplace to friends and family. The test: has the organisation's trajectory changed, or has it just delivered more of the same?

**How it can be reused:**
- **Talks:** Open any keynote with this definition. It immediately positions Jess as someone who thinks about transformation at a different level.
- **The book:** The opening chapter. "What transformation actually is" — and why getting it wrong is so expensive.
- **Client work:** Use as a diagnostic in the first conversation with any new client or sponsor. "What is your definition of transformation?" The answer tells you everything about the scope of work ahead.
- **GTL Programme:** The foundational module. Before any practice content, practitioners need to agree on what they are practising.
- **LinkedIn content:** A post structured around the three-word test: "Real. Adopted. Sustained. Not just launched."

**Soundbite:**
> "Transformation is a reality creation journey. You take a future worth having and make it real, adopted, and sustained — not just launched."

**Practical application:**
Apply the three-word test to any programme: (1) Is it real — has the new way of working been embedded in the organisation's day-to-day operations, or is it still being "rolled out"? (2) Is it adopted — are the people who need to work differently actually working differently, or have they been trained and left to figure it out? (3) Is it sustained — will this continue after the programme team leaves, or does it depend on the programme team to keep it alive?

**Tips, nuances, and edge cases:**
- The "soil" metaphor is particularly powerful with executives who are resistant to culture work. It reframes culture from "soft" to "foundational." You cannot grow a new reality in soil that will reject it.
- The "not just launched" addition to "real" is critical. Many programmes celebrate go-live as success. The definition explicitly excludes that.
- When using this definition with a sceptical audience, follow it immediately with the cost of misdefining: the compensation gap, the under-scoping, the eye-rolls in steering committees.

**Domain tags:**
Practice — the foundational definition of the discipline; also Positioning (how practitioners explain their work) and Leadership (what transformation leadership actually requires)

**Connections to other IP:**
- Blog 5 ("The missing layer") — the Conditions of Success Runway operationalises this definition. The 10 components are the practical expression of "prepare the soil, build capabilities, shift the trajectory."
- Blog 10 ("Change Management is boring") — the credibility problem described there is a direct consequence of the misdefining described here.
- Deepa Session 1 — "Transformation as Reality Creation" was articulated in that session (~18:00): "Transformation is a reality creation exercise. You are actually creating a reality for the organisation and for the people." This blog version is the written, developed form of that coaching moment.

**LinkedIn post idea:**
Hook: "Most organisations are not doing transformation. They are doing delivery with change support added on top."
Story: "I've watched the word 'transformation' get diluted until it means almost nothing. System implementations. Restructures. Branding refreshes. All called 'transformation.' The cost of that dilution is real: under-scoped work, under-resourced teams, under-delivered results."
Insight: "Transformation is a reality creation journey. You take a future worth having and make it real, adopted, and sustained — not just launched. Three words that change everything: real, adopted, sustained. Not just launched."
CTA: "Apply the test to your current programme. Is it real? Is it adopted? Is it sustained? Comment below — I'll tell you what I see."

### The Soil Metaphor — Culture as the Precondition for Transformation

**Source:** "So What Is Transformation Again?" blog | Section: "Prepare the soil" | transformationleadership.institute

**Verbatim transcript quote:**
> "Prepare the soil: every transformation and change lives or dies depending on the soil you plan to plant it in (the culture). These are the beliefs, mindset, incentives, ways of working, decision-making processes, the level of humaneness in the workplace, and power dynamics .. All so the new reality can flourish."

**What this IP is:**
A metaphor that makes the abstract concept of culture work immediately concrete and defensible. The soil metaphor reframes culture from "soft" to "foundational." It gives practitioners a way to explain why culture work is not optional — it is the precondition for everything else.

**Who it is for:**
Change practitioners who are struggling to get executives to invest in culture work. Sponsors who are asking "why do we need culture work — can't we just do the change?" Anyone who has watched a technically excellent programme fail because the culture rejected it.

**Pain point it addresses:**
The persistent belief that culture work is "soft," optional, or something that can be added on later. This belief leads to programmes that deliver technically but fail to adopt. The soil metaphor makes the case in a way that even the most delivery-focused executive can understand: you cannot grow a new reality in soil that will reject it.

**Benefits / value add:**
A memorable, defensible metaphor that practitioners can use in any conversation to make the case for culture work. It also gives a precise definition of what "culture" means in this context: beliefs, mindset, incentives, ways of working, decision-making processes, humaneness, and power dynamics. Not a vague "culture" but a specific set of conditions.

**The angle:**
The counterintuitive insight is that culture work is not about making people feel good — it is about creating the conditions in which the new reality can grow. The soil metaphor makes this engineering argument, not a human resources argument. That framing is more persuasive with delivery-focused executives.

**The framework:**
The soil metaphor has two components: (1) Diagnosis — what is the current state of the soil? What beliefs, mindsets, incentives, and power dynamics are present? Which of these will support the new reality, and which will reject it? (2) Preparation — what specific interventions will shift the soil conditions? Not a culture programme, but specific, targeted interventions tied to the transformation's requirements.

**How it can be reused:**
- **Talks:** A 2-minute segment in any keynote on transformation. The metaphor is visual and memorable.
- **Client work:** Use in the first conversation with any sponsor who is resistant to culture work.
- **GTL Programme:** The opening of the culture module in the Practice pillar.
- **The book:** A section in the "Prepare the soil" chapter.

**Soundbite:**
> "Every transformation lives or dies depending on the soil you plant it in. That soil is your culture."

**Practical application:**
Before any transformation programme begins, run a soil audit: What are the three beliefs in this organisation that will support the new reality? What are the three beliefs that will reject it? What are the incentives that currently reward the old way? What power dynamics will resist the change? The answers to these questions define the culture work required.

**Tips, nuances, and edge cases:**
- The soil metaphor works best when paired with a concrete example of a programme that failed because the soil was wrong. The "1,000-person organisation running 66 projects" example from this blog is a good one.
- The metaphor also works in reverse: a programme that succeeded because the soil was prepared. Ask the client: "Can you name a transformation that actually landed? What was different about the culture there?"

**Domain tags:**
Practice — culture as a precondition for transformation; also Leadership (what leaders must do to prepare the soil)

**Connections to other IP:**
- Blog 2 ("Change needs changing") — Shift 3 (no change without culture, no culture without change) is the practitioner-level expression of the same principle.
- Blog 5 ("The missing layer") — Component 5 (Culture parameters) in the Conditions of Success Runway is the operational expression of soil preparation.

**LinkedIn post idea:**
Hook: "Every transformation lives or dies depending on the soil you plant it in."
Story: "I've watched technically excellent programmes fail. New systems built. Training delivered. Communications sent. And yet, six months later, the old way of working is back. The programme didn't fail because of the technology or the plan. It failed because the soil rejected it."
Insight: "The soil is your culture: the beliefs, the incentives, the ways of working, the power dynamics. You cannot grow a new reality in soil that will reject it. Culture work is not soft. It is the precondition for everything else."
CTA: "What is the one belief in your organisation that is most likely to reject your current transformation? Name it below."

### The Credibility and Compensation Cost of Misdefining Transformation

**Source:** "So What Is Transformation Again?" blog | Section: "A wider set of consequences" | transformationleadership.institute

**Verbatim transcript quote:**
> "Compensation drops as transformation leaders are rebranded as glorified project/program managers and effort coordinators. I am heavily involved in the transformation talent space, as I help organisations build internal talent and hire strong transformation leaders. As I work across continents, mature markets, and organisations, I can see the pay gap, which is sometimes quite significant. Unfortunately, I see job ads here in Australia for transformation leaders with salaries of $150,000–$190,000 when, in fact, they should be at least $260,000."
> "Misreading what transformation is leads to under-scoping the work. The discipline is trivialised, and the 'transformation lead' is recast as admin: meetings, slides, wrangling, status."

**What this IP is:**
A direct, evidence-based argument for why the definition of transformation matters — not just philosophically, but financially and professionally. The credibility and compensation cost is a concrete, measurable consequence of misdefining the discipline.

**Who it is for:**
Transformation practitioners who are being underpaid or undervalued. Executives who are wondering why their transformation programmes are underperforming. HR leaders who are setting compensation benchmarks for transformation roles.

**Pain point it addresses:**
Transformation practitioners who are doing strategic work but being paid and treated as project coordinators. The frustration of having real capability but being unable to demonstrate it because the organisation's definition of transformation does not create space for strategic work.

**Benefits / value add:**
A precise, evidence-based argument for why transformation practitioners deserve more — in scope, in mandate, and in compensation. It also gives practitioners a language for the conversation with their sponsors: "If you define transformation as project management, you will get project management results."

**The angle:**
The counterintuitive insight is that the compensation gap is not a talent problem — it is a definition problem. Organisations that misdefine transformation create roles that attract and reward the wrong capabilities. The solution is not to find better people; it is to redefine the role.

**The framework:**
The cost cascade: (1) Misdefine transformation as delivery + change support. (2) Under-scope the work — the discipline is trivialised. (3) Recast the transformation lead as admin: meetings, slides, wrangling, status. (4) Compensation drops — the role is benchmarked against project management, not strategic leadership. (5) The best practitioners leave or never join. (6) The programme underperforms. (7) The reputation of transformation as a discipline takes a further hit. (8) Repeat.

**How it can be reused:**
- **Talks:** A 3-minute segment in any keynote on the future of the transformation profession.
- **LinkedIn content:** A post on the compensation gap — with the specific numbers from Australia.
- **Client work:** Use with a sponsor who is under-investing in transformation capability.
- **GTL Programme:** The opening of the Positioning pillar — why your definition of your role determines your compensation.

**Soundbite:**
> "I see job ads in Australia for transformation leaders with salaries of $150,000–$190,000 when, in fact, they should be at least $260,000. That gap is not a talent problem. It is a definition problem."

**Practical application:**
Run the definition test: Ask your sponsor, "What is your definition of transformation?" If the answer is "a large project with change support," you have a definition problem. The next conversation is about what transformation actually is — and what that means for scope, mandate, and compensation.

**Tips, nuances, and edge cases:**
- The specific salary numbers ($150K–$190K vs. $260K+) are powerful because they are concrete. Use them when speaking to Australian audiences. For other markets, acknowledge that the gap exists globally even if the numbers differ.
- The compensation argument is most powerful when paired with the ROI argument: under-investing in transformation capability costs the organisation far more than the compensation gap.

**Domain tags:**
Positioning — the professional and financial consequences of misdefining the discipline; also Practice

**Connections to other IP:**
- Blog 10 ("Change Management is boring") — the credibility problem described there is the public-facing version of the compensation problem described here.
- Blog 9 ("An invitation to go PRO in 2026") — the GTL Method and ELEVATE programme are the solution to the compensation and credibility gap described here.

**LinkedIn post idea:**
Hook: "Transformation leaders in Australia are being paid $150,000–$190,000 for work that should command at least $260,000. That gap is not a talent problem. It is a definition problem."
Story: "I've been in the transformation talent space for years — helping organisations hire and develop transformation leaders across continents. The pay gap is real. And it is not because the practitioners are underperforming. It is because the organisations have defined transformation as project management with change support added on top."
Insight: "When you misdefine transformation, you under-scope the work, trivialise the discipline, and recast the transformation lead as admin. The compensation follows the definition. Fix the definition, and the compensation follows."
CTA: "What is your organisation's definition of transformation? Be honest. I'll tell you what it is costing you."

## LINKEDIN POSTS — READY TO SCHEDULE

### Post 1 — "What Transformation Actually Is" (Definition Post)

**Hook:**
Most organisations call everything transformation. Very few are actually doing it.

Here's the distinction that matters.

**Body:**
Transformation is not a large project. It is not a system implementation. It is not a restructure.

Transformation is a shift in how an organisation sees itself, operates, and creates value — at the identity level, not just the process level.

There are three levels of change: process and technology (what you do and the tools you use), behaviour and culture (how you work and how you relate), and identity and purpose (who you are and why you exist).

Most organisations call it transformation when they're operating at level one. They implement a new system, restructure a team, or launch a new product — and they call it transformation.

Real transformation requires movement at all three levels simultaneously. And that's why it's hard. Not because the technology is complex. Because identity is hard to shift.

The test: after this programme, will the organisation see itself differently? Will it operate from a different set of assumptions? Will it create value in a fundamentally different way?

If the answer is yes — that's transformation. If the answer is "we'll have a new system" — that's a project.

**CTA:**
Which level is your current programme actually operating at? Be honest. The answer changes everything about how you resource it.

---

### Post 2 — "The Conditions of Success" (Diagnostic Post)

**Hook:**
Your transformation programme is behind schedule.

This is not a delivery problem.

**Body:**
Before any transformation programme begins, there are conditions that must be true. Not nice-to-have. Must be true.

If they're not in place, delivery will be slower, harder, and more expensive than it needs to be. Every time.

The conditions include: a shared, precise definition of what success looks like (not just "go-live"), alignment on what the organisation is becoming, behaviour and culture design embedded from the start, leadership norms that are explicit, and capacity that is real — not theoretical.

Most programmes skip the conditions because they feel like "soft" work. They feel like delay.

They're not delay. They're the difference between a programme that lands and one that has to be relaunched.

The surgeon who understands anatomy doesn't operate more slowly. They operate better, with fewer complications and less rework.

**CTA:**
Before your next programme kicks off, run a 10-minute conditions check. I'll share the questions if you want them — just comment below.

---

### Post 3 — "The 80/20 Flip" (Perspective Post)

**Hook:**
Most organisations have flipped the 80/20 balance.

They're spending 80% on doing and 20% on understanding. It should be the other way around.

**Body:**
When I work with organisations in the middle of transformation, I often see the same pattern.

Everyone is moving. Everyone is busy. The delivery machine is running. And nobody has stopped to ask: do we actually understand what we're transforming into?

The 80/20 flip means: before you add more, subtract. Before you launch the next initiative, decompress from the last one. Before you build the new capability, understand what the current system is actually doing.

Decompression is not avoidance. It is the precondition for honest decisions.

When you skip it, you get: resistance that was entirely predictable, rework that was entirely preventable, and a team that is exhausted before the hard work begins.

One or two humane, fundamental changes that put the organisation on the right path will always outperform ten stacked initiatives that are fighting each other for attention.

**CTA:**
What would you subtract from your current programme if you were honest? That subtraction might be the most important move you make this year.

---

## The Stacked Projects Critique, the Shock-to-System Principle, and the Invisible Adoption Gap (Blog IP)
**Description:** IP extraction from the blog 'Change is a Shock to the System'. Extracts the shock-to-system principle, the stacked projects critique, and the invisible adoption gap. Includes soundbites, LinkedIn post drafts, and book chapter candidates.

# Change as a Shock to the System, the 80/20 Decompression Principle, and Subtraction Before Addition — IP Extraction
## Blog: "Change is a Shock to the System" | transformationleadership.institute
**Source context:** A LinkedIn newsletter blog post by Jess Tayel that opens with a personal experience — a recurrence of a shoulder injury requiring invasive osteopathic treatment — and draws a direct parallel to how organisations experience change. The blog argues for an 80/20 inversion: 80% realisation and space, 20% how-to. Written in a personal, direct voice that bridges the human and organisational dimensions of change.
**Dominant theme:** Change as a shock to an existing system — the 80/20 decompression principle
**Secondary themes:** Subtraction before addition; the flipped 80/20 as the root cause of most transformation failure; decompression as a precondition for honest decisions; the human cost of over-change
**Sessions this theme also appears in:** Blog 4 is the primary source. The decompression principle connects to Blog 6 ("Permission to be more human") and Blog 8 ("My last note of 2025").
**Extracted by:** Manus AI | March 2026
**Confidential — Jess Internal Document**

---

### Change as a Shock to the System — The 80/20 Decompression Principle

**Source:** "Change is a Shock to the System" blog | transformationleadership.institute

**Verbatim transcript quote:**
> "I need 80% realisation and space, and 20% how-to. The same ratio would also serve many men, because this is a human condition. We all need more humane time and space, more heart and soul, and less pressure to grind through with our brains and bodies alone."
> "I'm arguing for the same 80/20 shift inside organisations. If we prioritise realising, decompressing, and creating space, the 20% that follows will be far more on point. We don't need countless changes. We need one or two humane, fundamental changes that put us on the right path."

**What this IP is:**
A framework that reframes the root cause of most transformation failure. The problem is not that organisations are doing the wrong things — it is that they have flipped the 80/20 ratio. They are spending 80% of their energy on "what to do" and 20% on "realising what is actually happening." The inversion of that ratio — 80% realisation and space, 20% how-to — is the precondition for change that actually lands.

**Who it is for:**
Leaders who are exhausted by change and cannot understand why their programmes are not working despite significant effort. Practitioners who are watching their technically correct work fail to produce adoption. Executives who are adding more change to solve the problems created by previous change.

**Pain point it addresses:**
The exhaustion and resistance that accumulates when organisations add change on top of change without creating space for people to decompress, realise, and recharge. The typical response to this exhaustion — restructuring, redundancies, more change — makes the problem worse. The 80/20 inversion explains why: you are asking people to absorb more shock before they have recovered from the last one.

**Benefits / value add:**
A precise diagnosis of why change is not landing, and a counterintuitive prescription: do less, create more space, and the 20% that follows will be far more effective. This is a powerful reframe for leaders who believe the answer to underperforming change is more change.

**The angle:**
The counterintuitive insight is that the most productive thing a leader can do when change is not landing is to stop adding change and create space for decompression. This feels wrong — it feels like giving up or slowing down. But the 80/20 principle explains why it is actually the fastest path to adoption: decompressed people make better decisions, take sharper actions, and own the change rather than resisting it.

**The framework:**
The 80/20 Decompression Principle has three components:

1. **Name the season.** Paint a picture of the current landscape — what feels heavy, where there is uncertainty, what is more difficult than it should be, where support is missing, where clarity is craved. Stop pretending everything is OK. Stop catastrophising. Get a solid, honest understanding of what the team collectively sees and feels.

2. **Create private rooms for real talk.** Give individuals protected time to say what is true without fixing it in the same breath. Give teams regular circles where the goal is to tell the truth and be heard — before anyone proposes a plan.

3. **Subtract before adding.** Remove one commitment before adding another. Make the removal visible so people see the weight coming off, not just the weight being moved around. Not everything requires a change programme and a culture overhaul.

When these three things happen, the team returns to work with cleaner intent. The next actions are smaller and sharper. The strategy is more focused. The meetings are shorter. The energy is redirected into impactful work instead of being wasted on performance around the work.

**How it can be reused:**
- **Talks:** A keynote segment that opens with the personal story (shoulder injury, osteopathic needles) and draws the parallel to organisations. The personal story makes the abstract concrete.
- **The book:** A chapter titled "The 80/20 Inversion — Why More Change Is Making Your Change Worse."
- **Client work:** Use as a diagnostic when a programme is stalling. "Have you created space for decompression, or have you added more change to solve the problem?"
- **GTL Programme:** A module in the Leadership pillar — the leader's role in creating decompression space.
- **LinkedIn content:** A post structured around the three decompression practices.

**Soundbite:**
> "We don't need countless changes. We need one or two humane, fundamental changes that put us on the right path. Currently, we are doing the opposite — and we are feeling the cost."

**Practical application:**
Run the 80/20 audit with your team: What percentage of our current energy is going to "what to do" vs. "understanding what is actually happening"? If the ratio is inverted, identify one thing to subtract before adding anything new. Name the season the team is in — honestly, without fixing it in the same breath.

**Tips, nuances, and edge cases:**
- The personal opening (shoulder injury) is a deliberate choice — it makes the abstract concept of "shock to the system" visceral and immediate. Use it when speaking to audiences who are resistant to "soft" change concepts.
- The 80/20 principle is not an argument for doing nothing. It is an argument for doing less, better. The 20% that follows decompression is more targeted, more owned, and more likely to stick.
- The "seasons, not sprints" language is particularly powerful with teams that are in a perpetual sprint culture. It gives them permission to name a different kind of time.

**Domain tags:**
Leadership — the leader's role in creating decompression space; also Practice (the architecture of change that accounts for human capacity) and Wellbeing (the human cost of over-change)

**Connections to other IP:**
- Blog 6 ("Permission to be more human") — the decompression principle is the organisational expression of the same argument Blog 6 makes at the individual level.
- Blog 8 ("My last note of 2025") — the "clean finish" and "subtraction before addition" themes connect directly.
- Blog 5 ("The missing layer") — Component 9 (Team capacity and capability) in the Conditions of Success Runway is the operational expression of the decompression principle.

**LinkedIn post idea:**
Hook: "The most productive thing you can do when your change is not landing is to stop adding change."
Story: "I know how counterintuitive that sounds. When a programme is underperforming, the instinct is to add more: more resources, more communications, more workshops, more change. But that instinct is making the problem worse. You are asking people to absorb more shock before they have recovered from the last one."
Insight: "Change is a shock to the system. Every change requires energy to absorb — cognitive, emotional, and physical energy. When you add change on top of change without creating space for decompression, you are not accelerating adoption. You are accumulating resistance. The 80/20 principle: 80% realisation and space, 20% how-to. Invert that ratio and watch what happens."
CTA: "What is the one thing your team needs to subtract before you add anything new? Name it below."

---

### Subtraction Before Addition

**Source:** "Change is a Shock to the System" blog | Section: "What decompression actually looks like" | transformationleadership.institute

**Verbatim transcript quote:**
> "Subtraction before addition. We remove one commitment before adding another, and we make the removal visible so people see the weight coming off, not just the weight being moved around. Not everything requires a change program and a culture overhaul!"

**What this IP is:**
A simple, actionable principle that operationalises the 80/20 decompression framework. It is not just a philosophical position — it is a specific practice that leaders can implement immediately. The visibility of the removal is as important as the removal itself: people need to see the weight coming off, not just the weight being moved around.

**Who it is for:**
Leaders who are adding new initiatives to already overloaded teams. Programme leads who are being asked to add scope without removing anything. Anyone who has watched a team's morale decline as the list of commitments grows.

**Pain point it addresses:**
The pattern of adding change without removing anything — which creates the illusion of progress while actually accumulating debt. The team is not doing less; they are doing more, with less capacity, and the quality of everything declines.

**Benefits / value add:**
A simple, visible practice that immediately demonstrates respect for the team's capacity. The visibility of the removal is a leadership signal: "I see the weight you are carrying, and I am taking something off before I add something new."

**The angle:**
The counterintuitive insight is that the act of visible removal is as important as the removal itself. Most leaders remove things quietly — they just stop asking for them. But the team does not know the weight has come off. Making the removal visible is a leadership practice that builds trust and creates the psychological space for the new commitment.

**The framework:**
Three steps: (1) Name the commitment being removed — specifically, not vaguely. Not "we'll deprioritise X" but "we are stopping X as of this week." (2) Make the removal visible — announce it in the same forum where new commitments are announced. (3) Wait before adding — give the team time to feel the weight coming off before adding the new commitment.

**How it can be reused:**
- **Talks:** A 2-minute segment in any keynote on leadership and change.
- **Client work:** Use as a leadership coaching practice with executives who are overloading their teams.
- **GTL Programme:** A module in the Leadership pillar — the practice of visible subtraction.
- **LinkedIn content:** A standalone post on "subtraction before addition."

**Soundbite:**
> "We remove one commitment before adding another, and we make the removal visible so people see the weight coming off — not just the weight being moved around."

**Practical application:**
Before your next team meeting where you plan to announce a new initiative, identify one existing commitment that can be removed or paused. Announce the removal first. Watch the energy in the room change.

**Tips, nuances, and edge cases:**
- The most common objection: "We can't remove anything — everything is critical." The response: "If everything is critical, nothing is. The inability to remove anything is itself a symptom of the problem."
- The visibility of the removal matters most in cultures where leaders are seen as adding without removing. In those cultures, a visible removal is a significant leadership signal.

**Domain tags:**
Leadership — the practice of visible subtraction; also Practice (the architecture of change that accounts for human capacity)

**Connections to other IP:**
- Blog 7 ("A Clean Finish to the Year") — the "clean finish" principle is the annual expression of subtraction before addition.
- Blog 5 ("The missing layer") — Component 9 (Team capacity and capability) — the subtraction practice is one of the tools for managing real capacity.

**LinkedIn post idea:**
Hook: "Before you announce your next initiative, remove one thing."
Story: "Not quietly. Not 'we'll deprioritise X.' Remove it. Name it. Make it visible. Let the team feel the weight coming off before you add the new weight."
Insight: "Subtraction before addition is not a soft principle. It is a leadership practice. It says: I see what you are carrying. I am not going to add to it without taking something off first. That act of visible removal builds more trust than any engagement survey."
CTA: "What is the one thing your team is still carrying that no longer needs to be carried? Name it."

### The Flipped 80/20 as the Root Cause of Transformation Failure

**Source:** "Change is a Shock to the System" blog | Section: "Are we contributing to making things more difficult than they need to be?" | transformationleadership.institute

**Verbatim transcript quote:**
> "I don't know where in the history of change or transformation it became a set of stacked projects and tactics rather than a co-designed experience, with cognitive and emotional culture levers, an integrated ecosystem thinking, and a definition of success at a 360-degree level."
> "Some organisations and leaders make change much more complicated than it is. By: Adding too much change. Providing the team with a high-level 'north star' and hoping they will figure it out. Not investing time in seeing the organisation as a single, interconnected continuum."
> "It won't work even if it appears to be working; the long-term price will have to be paid, and it is expensive."

**What this IP is:**
A diagnosis of the root cause of most transformation failure: organisations are making change more complicated than it needs to be by adding too much, moving too fast, and failing to see the organisation as an interconnected system. The "stacked projects and tactics" framing is a precise description of what most organisations call transformation.

**Who it is for:**
Executives who are frustrated that their transformation programmes are not producing the results they expected. Practitioners who are watching their technically correct work fail to produce adoption. Anyone who has been in a steering committee where the programme is "on track" but the organisation is clearly struggling.

**Pain point it addresses:**
The frustration of doing everything "right" — having the right methodology, the right resources, the right plan — and still not seeing results. The diagnosis: the problem is not the execution. It is the architecture. Stacked projects and tactics cannot produce transformation. Only a co-designed experience with integrated culture levers can.

**Benefits / value add:**
A precise diagnosis that gives practitioners and executives a language for what is going wrong. It also gives a clear prescription: stop adding, start integrating. See the organisation as a single, interconnected continuum. Define success at a 360-degree level, not just at go-live.

**The angle:**
The counterintuitive insight is that the symptoms of transformation failure — resistance, passive-aggressive behaviour, quiet quitting, going in circles — are not caused by the people. They are caused by the architecture. The answer is not consultants, doubling down, restructures, or leadership development programmes. The answer is to stop flipping the 80/20 ratio.

**The framework:**
The root cause cascade: (1) Add too much change. (2) Provide a high-level north star and hope the team figures it out. (3) Fail to see the organisation as a single, interconnected continuum. (4) Fail to define what good looks like at a 360-degree level. (5) Fail to take behavioural change and culture seriously — with depth, integrated from day one. (6) Symptoms appear: resistance, passive-aggressive behaviour, quiet quitting, going in circles, heightened team dynamics, leadership disconnection. (7) The response is more of the same: consultants, doubling down, restructures. (8) The long-term price is paid — and it is expensive.

**How it can be reused:**
- **Talks:** A keynote segment on "why transformation fails — and it's not what you think."
- **The book:** A chapter on the root cause of transformation failure.
- **Client work:** Use as a diagnostic when a programme is stalling or producing symptoms.
- **GTL Programme:** The opening of the Practice pillar — what co-designed transformation looks like vs. stacked projects and tactics.

**Soundbite:**
> "It won't work even if it appears to be working. The long-term price will have to be paid — and it is expensive."

**Practical application:**
Run the integration audit: Is your transformation a co-designed experience, or a set of stacked projects and tactics? Do you have cognitive and emotional culture levers integrated into the delivery work? Do you have a 360-degree definition of success? If the answers are no, you have a root cause problem, not an execution problem.

**Tips, nuances, and edge cases:**
- The "even if it appears to be working" line is particularly powerful. Many executives believe their programme is working because the milestones are being hit. The insight: hitting milestones is not the same as producing transformation. The long-term price will be paid.
- The list of symptoms (resistance, passive-aggressive behaviour, quiet quitting, going in circles) is a diagnostic checklist. If three or more are present, the root cause is almost certainly the flipped 80/20.

**Domain tags:**
Practice — the architecture of transformation vs. stacked projects; also Leadership (what leaders must do differently) and Positioning (how practitioners explain the root cause to executives)

**Connections to other IP:**
- Blog 3 ("So What Is Transformation Again?") — the "stacked projects and tactics" framing is the negative definition of transformation. The positive definition (reality creation journey) is in Blog 3.
- Blog 5 ("The missing layer") — the Conditions of Success Runway is the positive prescription for the root cause diagnosed here.

**LinkedIn post idea:**
Hook: "Your transformation is producing symptoms: resistance, quiet quitting, going in circles. The answer is not consultants. It is not doubling down. It is not a restructure."
Story: "I've watched organisations respond to transformation failure with more of the same. More resources. More change. More pressure. And the symptoms get worse. Because the problem is not the execution. It is the architecture."
Insight: "Change is a shock to the system. Every change requires energy to absorb. When you stack projects and tactics without integrating culture levers, without a 360-degree definition of success, without seeing the organisation as a single interconnected continuum — you are creating a shock that the system cannot absorb. It won't work even if it appears to be working. The long-term price will be paid. And it is expensive."
CTA: "Which of these symptoms is your programme producing right now? Be honest."

## LINKEDIN POSTS — READY TO SCHEDULE

### Post 1 — "Change as Shock to the System" (Core Concept Post)

**Hook:**
Your organisation is not resisting change.

It is responding to shock.

**Body:**
Every time you add a new initiative, a new system, a new way of working — you are sending a shock through an existing system.

That system has its own rhythm. Its own habits. Its own immune response.

The shock is not the problem. The shock is the signal.

It tells you: the system is not ready for this. Not because the people are bad. Because the conditions are not in place.

Most organisations respond to the shock by adding more: more communications, more training, more change resources, more pressure on the timeline.

That is not change management. That is compounding the shock.

The right response is different: understand the system before you shock it. Design the conditions of success before you begin delivery. Give the system what it needs to absorb the change — not just what the project plan requires.

**CTA:**
What shock is your organisation currently absorbing? And what would it need to absorb it well? Those two questions are the start of real change design.

---

### Post 2 — "Stacked Projects" (Provocation Post)

**Hook:**
The problem isn't that your people can't handle change.

The problem is that you've stacked 12 changes on top of each other and called it a transformation programme.

**Body:**
I keep seeing this.

An organisation launches a transformation. Then another initiative. Then a restructure. Then a technology implementation. Then a culture programme. All running simultaneously. All competing for the same people's attention, energy, and cognitive bandwidth.

And then the organisation is surprised when adoption is low, morale is down, and the "transformation" is behind schedule.

This is not a change management problem. This is a portfolio design problem.

The antidote: subtraction before addition. Remove one commitment before adding another. Make the removal visible so people see the weight coming off, not just the weight being moved around.

Not everything requires a change programme and a culture overhaul. Sometimes the most transformative thing you can do is stop.

**CTA:**
How many parallel initiatives is your team currently running? Count them. Then ask: which two are actually moving the needle? The rest might be the problem.

---

### Post 3 — "Decompression Before Direction" (Leadership Post)

**Hook:**
Before you plan the next phase of your transformation, your team needs to decompress from this one.

**Body:**
Decompression is not a luxury. It is the precondition for honest decisions.

When teams skip it, they carry the weight of the last phase into the next one. The unresolved frustrations, the unanswered questions, the quiet resentments — they don't disappear. They show up as resistance, disengagement, and quiet quitting.

What decompression looks like in practice: name the season you're in (what feels heavy, where is the team unsure, what is harder than it should be), create protected time to say what is true without fixing it in the same breath, and subtract before you add — remove one commitment before adding another.

When you do this, the next phase starts cleaner. The strategy is more focused. The energy is redirected into impactful work instead of being wasted on performance around the work.

**CTA:**
When did your team last have a genuine decompression conversation — not a retrospective, not a lessons-learned session, but a real "how are we actually doing?" moment? If you can't remember, that's the answer.

---

# The Conditions of Success Runway — 10 Components, the Surgeon Analogy, and the Shaping Phase Principle — IP Extraction
## Blog: "The missing layer in your 2026 plan" | transformationleadership.institute
**Source context:** A LinkedIn newsletter blog post by Jess Tayel that opens with a surgeon analogy and introduces the "Conditions of Success Runway" — a 10-component framework for what must be designed before and during transformation delivery. Includes a self-check scorecard. Written as a practical, diagnostic tool for transformation leaders planning their 2026 programmes.
**Dominant theme:** The Conditions of Success Runway — the 10 pre-conditions for transformation success
**Secondary themes:** The surgeon analogy (preparation as the source of outcomes); the shaping phase principle (if you skip shaping, you are not saving time); the distinction between delivery and design
**Sessions this theme also appears in:** Blog 2 ("Change needs changing") — the Five Shifts are the practitioner-level expression of the same principles; Blog 3 ("So What Is Transformation Again?") — the three components of real transformation (soil, capabilities, trajectory) map directly to the Runway components
**Extracted by:** Manus AI | March 2026
**Confidential — Jess Internal Document**

---

### The Conditions of Success Runway — 10 Components

**Source:** "The missing layer in your 2026 plan" blog | transformationleadership.institute

**Verbatim transcript quote:**
> "Ask any surgeon: the most critical work in a surgery often happens before and outside the theatre: diagnosis, consent, the team brief, the full design of all the scenarios, readiness, and the step-by-step recovery plan. This is what reduces the risks, gives a solid way forward, and predicts progress with confidence."
> "Transformation works the same way. Outcomes come from the conditions you set before kickoff and maintain throughout the process. I call this the Conditions of Success Runway."

**What this IP is:**
A 10-component framework that defines the conditions that must be designed before and maintained throughout any transformation programme. It is not a methodology — it is a pre-conditions framework. The distinction matters: methodologies organise delivery. The Runway organises the conditions in which delivery can succeed.

**Who it is for:**
Transformation leaders who are planning a programme and want to ensure they are not setting themselves up for the adoption failure that comes from skipping the shaping phase. Executives who are sponsoring transformation and want to understand what they need to provide for the programme to succeed. Practitioners who are mid-programme and want to diagnose why adoption is lower than expected.

**Pain point it addresses:**
The pattern of jumping from strategy to delivery without designing the conditions in which delivery can succeed. Most programmes skip or rush the shaping phase because it feels like it is not "real work." The result is delivery that is weighed down by misalignment, unclear capabilities, culture that rejects the change, and teams that are overloaded before the programme starts.

**Benefits / value add:**
A complete, structured framework for the shaping phase. Each component is a specific design question that must be answered before delivery begins. The self-check scorecard makes it immediately actionable: any unchecked box becomes this week's or month's focus.

**The angle:**
The counterintuitive insight is that skipping the shaping phase does not save time — it costs time. The surgeon analogy makes this concrete: a surgeon who skips the pre-operative preparation is not saving time. They are creating complications that will cost far more time, risk, and resource to resolve. The same is true in transformation.

**The framework:**
The 10 Components of the Conditions of Success Runway:

1. **Alignment.** How aligned are we with the strategy? Choices? No-Gos? Are we on the same page with a definition of success and "done," scope guardrails, decision rights, and escalation paths? If you ask the sponsor and the CEO what the future looks like after this transformation, will you get the same answer?

2. **Business capability identification.** Name the 3–5 capabilities you are building, elevating, protecting, or retiring. Every work package in the plan maps to a capability and an outcome — not just a project or deliverable.

3. **Change enablement.** Adoption is designed in from day one: who must do what differently, how teams will be enabled, how communication will be personalised, what support they will get, and how resistance will be heard early.

4. **Leadership traits and norms.** How will leaders show up to guard this transformation? The few behaviours they will reward and refuse: decide in the room, say the hard thing early, protect focus, close loops.

5. **Culture parameters.** What to elevate (candour, cadence, behaviours, learning, accountability) and what to stop. Healthy rituals that make it real. Team values and how they translate to cognitive and emotional culture.

6. **Workforce planning.** Roles, skills, reporting lines, work arrangements, dynamics, capacity, expectations, and capability. Trade-offs, handovers, settling into ways of working, role expectations, boundaries, and personal development.

7. **Ways of working.** Business processes, business rules, supporting data, unsaid expectations, documentation, workflows, decision-making, and expectations. Ways of working do not exist in isolation — they need data, systems, communication, and KPIs.

8. **Success 360.** An integrated view of success across multiple lenses: customer experience, scalability, simplification, risk appetite, financials, and more. Every team has their own definition of progress that rolls up cleanly.

9. **Team capacity and capability.** Workload and real (mental and cognitive) capacity — the most-ignored area in transformation planning. What does each team member need to become more engaged, receptive, and own the change? Coaching, pausing current work, better remuneration, resolution of dynamics, or reskilling?

10. **Experience: internal and external.** The lived experience for employees, customers, and partners. Walk the end-to-end journey and design for friction removal and delight moment elevation.

**Priority note:** Component 1 (Alignment) is the highest priority. If it is not well defined, there is no point going further. Components 2, 5, and 8 are the next priority.

**How it can be reused:**
- **Workshops:** The Runway is a complete workshop module. Run the 10-question self-check with the programme team and use the gaps as the agenda.
- **Talks:** A keynote segment on "what the surgeon knows that most transformation leaders don't."
- **The book:** A chapter titled "The Conditions of Success Runway — What Must Be True Before Delivery Begins."
- **Client work:** Use as a programme health check at any stage. Any unchecked box is a risk to adoption.
- **GTL Programme:** A core module in the Practice pillar — the shaping phase as the source of outcomes.

**Soundbite:**
> "If you skip shaping and designing phases, you are not saving time. You are costing yourself and the organisation so much more time, lost opportunities, and a lot of pressure."

**Practical application:**
Run the 10-question self-check (15 minutes, tick only if the answer is a clear "yes"). Any unchecked box becomes this week's or month's focus. One owner per box, one visible move before the next milestone.

**Tips, nuances, and edge cases:**
- The most commonly skipped components are 5 (Culture parameters), 8 (Success 360), and 9 (Team capacity and capability). These are also the three that most directly predict adoption outcomes.
- Component 9 is "probably the most-ignored area in transformation planning" — Jess's own emphasis. Teams are routinely asked to absorb new work without any honest assessment of their actual capacity.
- The Runway is not a sequential checklist — components are interdependent. But Component 1 (Alignment) must be resolved first. Without alignment, the other components are built on sand.

**Domain tags:**
Practice — the shaping phase and pre-conditions for transformation success; also Leadership (Component 4 — leadership traits and norms) and Positioning (how practitioners explain the value of the shaping phase)

**Connections to other IP:**
- Blog 3 ("So What Is Transformation Again?") — the three components of real transformation (prepare the soil, build capabilities, shift the trajectory) map directly to the Runway: soil = Components 4, 5; capabilities = Components 2, 6, 7; trajectory = Component 8.
- Blog 2 ("Change needs changing") — the Five Shifts are the practitioner-level expression of the same principles. Shift 4 (start on day one) = Component 3. Shift 3 (no change without culture) = Component 5.
- Blog 4 ("Change is a Shock to the System") — Component 9 (Team capacity and capability) is the operational expression of the decompression principle.

**LinkedIn post idea:**
Hook: "Ask any surgeon: the most critical work happens before the theatre. Transformation works the same way."
Story: "I've watched programmes skip the shaping phase because it feels like it's not 'real work.' The result is always the same: delivery weighed down by misalignment, unclear capabilities, culture that rejects the change, and teams that are overloaded before the programme starts."
Insight: "I call it the Conditions of Success Runway. Ten components that must be designed before and maintained throughout delivery. The most ignored: team capacity and capability. The most critical: alignment. If Component 1 is not resolved, there is no point going further."
CTA: "Run the self-check: which of the 10 components is your programme missing? Comment below — I'll tell you what that gap is costing you."
